Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Crude climbs as Middle East escalation risks remain in focus
Hormuz transits climb to 26 despite attacks
Houthis claim to target Saudi refineries, Abqaiq site
Crude oil futures settled higher Oct. 30 as Middle East supply risks remained in focus amid Strait of Hormuz vessel attacks and continued Houthi strikes on Saudi infrastructure.
NYMEX December WTI settled $2.12/barrel higher at $104.12/b and ICE December Brent climbed $2.76/b to $117.64/b.
Traffic through the Strait of Hormuz totaled 26 ships on Oct. 29, including five crude tankers moving into the waterway, according to the latest report from S&P Global Commodities At Sea. The count ticked up from 18 transits the day before.
Ship attacks have picked up over the past few weeks in the contested waterway, and four incidents were reported by Iranian media Oct. 29 and one incident on Oct. 30.
Meanwhile, Yemen-based Houthi militants claimed a series of fresh missile strikes on Saudi Arabian energy facilities, including Abqaiq and two refineries, group spokesperson Ameen Hayyan said on X late Oct. 29.
The Houthis claimed strikes on the 400,000 b/d Rabigh refinery and the 550,000 b/d Ras Tanura refinery, as well as Aramco facilities in Abqaiq, without specifying what was targeted.
Aramco did not respond to a request for comment. NYMEX November RBOB settled 12.70 cents/gallon higher at $3.3120/gal and November ULSD climbed 5.45 cents/gal higher to $4.7905/gal. "Another escalation would hit a market with fewer shock absorbers than at the start of the conflict," ANZ Commodity

International Crude
Atlantic Basin crude differentials near record lows as Europe stays well supplied
European crude supply length pressures prices
Freight costs limit Latin American exports
Guyanese, Brazilian, WAF differentials hit lows
Atlantic Basin crude differentials remained near record lows as Europe was well supplied with short-haul barrels and prohibitive freight weighed on FOB differentials at the end of the month.
From Latin America and the Caribbean, freight remained a prohibitive factor for crude landing in Europe, despite some softening on the long-haul routes from both Guyana and Brazil.
Platts assessed the runs of a Suezmax on the Guyana-UK Continent route at $120.33/metric ton Oct. 29, down $51.57/mt from the record high of $171.90/mt, reached multiple times in October, most recently on Oct. 13. A similar route from Brazil to the UK Continent was assessed at $144.97/mt Oct. 29, down from the record high of $203.99/mt reached in October.
"Freight and structure [are] running away a bit again and short haul diffs keep coming off," said one Europe-based trader familiar with grades across the basin.
Indeed, local European sweet crudes have seen prices move lower in recent weeks as supply lengthened in the Mediterranean and North Sea and buyers' options for alternatives remained widely available.
Platts assessed CPC Blend CIF Augusta at a $10.195/barrel discount to Dated Brent on Oct. 29, down by more than $14/b since the beginning of October.
Meanwhile, all four Guyanese crude grades fell to their weakest differentials against the Latin American Dated Brent strip on Oct. 26 and held steady until the Oct. 29 session, when they narrowed by $1/barrel to the strip, reversing a downtrend that had endured throughout October as the majority of the November loading program traded.
Platts assessed Guyana's flagship Liza crude on an FOB basis at a discount of $29/b to the Latin American Dated Brent strip Oct. 29, as it gained in line with strengthening movements of competing West African grades.
In Brazil, Mero crude, which also typically lands in Europe, fell to its lowest differential against the Latin American Dated Brent strip during the Oct. 29 session, as indications for December

Platts assessed Mero FOB at a discount of $27.70/b to the Dated Brent strip Oct. 29, down $1.25/b day over day.
Earlier in the month, Brazilian grades also struggled to secure placements at other global destinations, especially in China.
"China has not been buying anything for a month," said one Brazil-based trader.
While there has been some recent clearance, competition from similar-quality crudes within the basin and the availability of alternative barrels have continued to pressure differentials across the basin.
The softness observed in the Brazilian and Guyanese markets followed a sharp decline in FOB cash differentials in West Africa, a key competitive long-haul market.
While Nigerian crudes benefited from domestic demand in the form of the Dangote refinery, eastern-facing crudes suffered from lackluster Chinese buying, pooling supply in the West of Suez region.
Platts assessed Congolese grade Djeno as low as a $34.70/ barrel discount to Dated Brent on a FOB basis in October, its alltime low.
"It really is the worst I have ever seen in any of the three markets... just feels like a race to the bottom. Add to that new Guyana production coming online in Dec and it is very difficult to get constructive even from these levels," said the same Europebased trader.
With Guyana's newest Uaru Gold crude entering the market in December, concerns about oversupply continued to weigh on market sentiment.
-- Jada Johnson, George Delaney, Jan Kedzior

Saudi Arabia has not publicly commented on the latest attacks claimed by the Houthis, but Saudi Civil Defense issued overnight warnings of incoming airstrikes in multiple areas of the country, including the Eastern Province, where the Abqaiq facilities and the Ras Tanura refinery are located.
The January ICE Brent futures contract spiked to $114.06/b at market open on Oct. 30, the highest since May 5. By 0900 GMT, the contract was trading at $112.56/b, up 1.6% from the previous close.

Crude jumps after Houthis claim strikes on Saudi Abqaiq facilities, oil refineries
Brent crude spikes to near six-month high Rabigh, Ras Tanura refineries targeted Abqaiq processes 5% of the world's oil

Yemen-based Houthi militants claimed a series of fresh missile strikes on Saudi Arabian energy facilities, including Abqaiq and two refineries, group spokesperson Ameen Hayyan said on X late Oct. 29.
The Houthis claimed strikes on the 400,000 barrels/day Rabigh refinery and the 550,000 b/d Ras Tanura refinery, as well as Aramco facilities in Abqaiq, without specifying what was targeted there. Processing facilities at Abqaiq have been responsible for around 5% of the world's oil supply, according to information on Aramco's website. The facilities are located at the eastern end of the 7 million b/d East-West pipeline that carries crude to the Red Sea port of Yanbu.
NASA's satellite-based fire-detection mapping platform, FIRMS, did not appear to show any abnormal fires at the Abqaiq facilities in images captured at 02:25 GMT on Oct. 30. The closest fires detected in the last 24 hours were some 6.5 km from the

Strikes on Saudi Arabian energy infrastructure have intensified recently, with the Houthis claiming a series of attacks. The Houthis recently said they had struck the Ras Tanura oil refinery.
The Ras Tanura refinery was also targeted in early April, along with several other refineries, and twice in March, Platts reported earlier.
In the Oct. 29 attacks, the Houthis also claimed strikes on Riyadh's King Khalid International Airport, King Fahd International Airport in Dammam and military installations in the Najran region. Saudi Civil Defense issued overnight warnings of airstrikes in Najran and Riyadh.
Abqaiq was targeted in a September 2019 attack that temporarily took about 5.7 million b/d of Saudi crude production offline and initially sent crude prices up 20%.
-- Lauren Holtmeier

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 30, 2026

Platts December cash Dubai crude closes at $118.70/b, averages $110.731/b in October
Cash Oman closes at $118.70/b; averages at $110.818/b
Dubai cash-futures spread averages $8.10/b in October
24 convergence cargoes declared in October
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed benchmark December cash Dubai crude at $118.70/barrel Oct. 30, the last day of trading for December-loading Middle East crude.
December cash Dubai assessments averaged $110.731/b in October, down from an average of $113.711/b for front-month November cash Dubai in September, Platts data showed.
December cash Oman crude was assessed at $118.70/b Oct. 30, averaging $110.818/b in October, down from an average of $113.767/b for front-month November cash Oman in September.
The Dubai cash-futures spread, a measure of the market structure, weakened to average $8.10/b in October, down from $21.60/b in September.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process on Oct. 30 saw 56 partials traded, all of which were for December Dubai partials. The sellers were Mitsui, BP, Unipec, Glencore, Centalion and Mercuria, while the buyers were Trafigura, ExxonMobil and PetroChina.
There were two convergences on Oct. 30, with Mercuria and Centalion each declaring a December Murban cargo to Trafigura.
A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between

two counterparties in the same direction, resulting in a full 500,000-barrel physical cargo being declared from the seller to the buyer.
For Dubai and Oman partials, the seller has the option to deliver Oman or Murban crude with a quality adjustment to the buyer.
In October, a total of 610 partials were traded, all of which were for December Dubai partials.
On the buy side, Trafigura led with 528 partials, followed by ExxonMobil with 58 partials, while BP and Phillips 66 each had seven partials.
On the sell side, Unipec led with 209 partials, followed by Mercuria with 84 partials and BP with 81 partials.
A total of 24 December convergence cargoes were declared during the month, consisting of 17 Oman crude cargoes and seven Murban crude cargoes.
Trafigura was the receiver of 23 of those cargoes, while ExxonMobil received one cargo.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 30, 2026

GME Dec Oman crude oil contract settles at $116.32/b, averages $110.455/b
Dec contract average falls
Nov contract average was $114.067/b
The Gulf Mercantile Exchange's December Oman crude oil futures contract settled at $116.32/barrel on Oct. 30, the last trading day of the month, according to exchange data on the GME website.
The December Oman contract averaged $110.455/b in October, compared with an average of $114.067/b for the November contract in September, the data showed.
Oman's oil ministry uses the average GME Oman settlement price to set the value of its term export cargoes, according to the exchange's website.
-- Alvinn Philips
SINGAPORE DATA: Crude imports fall 41% week over week to 662,422 million mt
Middle East accounts for 82% of oil imports
Saudi condensate imported over latest week
Singapore's crude oil imports fell 41.2% week over week to 662,422 metric tons over Oct. 22-28, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 29.
The Middle East accounted for 82% of total crude oil imports, with 271,313 mt from Oman, 138,487 mt from Saudi Arabia, and 133,099 mt from the UAE, according to the data.
Imports were also recorded from the US at 46,425 mt, South Sudan at 35,636 mt, Canada at 30,264 mt, Papua New Guinea at 7,193 mt, and Japan at 4.8 mt, the data showed.
Saudi Arabia constituted the sole source of condensate imports for the week, with 120,925 mt recorded from the producer.
Singapore's crude oil imports averaged 113,077 mt/d over Oct. 1-28, up from 99,070 mt/d in September, according to Enterprise Singapore data compiled by Platts.
The city-state's condensate imports averaged 13,412 mt/d over Oct. 1-28, up from 9,824 mt/d in September, the data showed.
-- Yong ren Toh
CASH BFOE EXPIRY ROUNDUP: Two December convergence crude cargoes trade in MOC
149 partials trade across Sep, Oct
Two convergences result from trading activity
The Platts Market on Close assessment process saw two December Cash BFOE convergence crude cargoes trading between September and October, data from Platts, part of S&P

Global Energy, showed Oct. 30. Some 149 December partials, each equivalent to 100,000
barrels, were traded. Mercuria was the most active participant on the buy side of
the prompt Cash BFOE partial contract during the MOC, while Petroineos was the most active seller.
Mercuria bought 48 December partials, followed by Petroineos with 33 and Vitol with 21.
On the sell side, Petroineos sold 44 December partials, followed by Mercuria with 33 and Vitol with 22.
No full 700,000-barrel Cash BFOE cargoes traded for December delivery across September and October.
Trading activity from September to October resulted in two convergence cargoes, up from no convergences the previous month. Glencore and BP were on the sell side, while Shell and Mercuria were on the buy side.
Convergence on the BFOE forward contract occurs when a company reaches a net position of seven 100,000-barrel partials with another company.
Under the terms of the trading contract, the seller has the option to declare a 700,000-barrel cargo of Brent/Ninian Blend, Forties, Oseberg, Ekofisk, Troll or West Texas Intermediate Midland crude to the buyer a month before its loading or arrival in Rotterdam for WTI Midland.
-- Ernest Puey, Natasha Tan
SOUTH KOREA DATA: Jan-Sept Americas crude imports hit record 191 million barrels
Americas supply 26% of South Korea's crude needs
Platts assesses WTI Midland below than Murban CFR Asia
Canadian heavy grades fill Basrah Medium, Arab Heavy gaps
South Korea secured 191.4 million barrels of crude from suppliers in the Americas over January-September, marking record-high shipments from the region for the first three quarters, as Asia's top US crude buyer extended purchases alongside ample Canadian heavy crude inflows, according to industry sources, state-run Korea National Oil Corp., and the trade ministry.
The Americas accounted for 26.12% of South Korea's crude arrivals during the first three quarters, compared with 23.1% in 2025 and 21.6% in 2024, KNOC data issued Oct. 28 showed.
The rising share reflects refiners' efforts to diversify feedstock sources amid persistent geopolitical and logistical risks surrounding traditional Middle Eastern supply routes. Regular US export programs have provided dependable light sweet US barrels, while Canadian heavy sour grades have helped offset constrained availability of Arab Medium, Arab Heavy and other heavy Middle Eastern crudes, feedstock managers at three major refiners based in Ulsan, Seosan, Yeosu told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, during market discussions over Oct 28-30.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 30, 2026

In total, South Korea - Asia's third-biggest crude buyer imported 81.22 million barrels of crude in September, up 2.9% from 78.92 million barrels a year earlier, despite the tanker flow disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz, according to KNOC data released Oct. 28 and the second-half 2026 industry report issued by the country's Ministry of Trade, Industry and Resources.
US, Canadian flows South Korea's imports of US crude reached 13.13 million
barrels in September, up 17.5% year over year, KNOC data showed. Cumulative US shipments rose 19.1% to 144.23 million barrels during January-September, cementing the country's position as South Korea's second-largest crude supplier after Saudi Arabia.

Demand for Cold Lake and Access Western Blend has strengthened because their heavy sour qualities make them useful substitutes for Basrah Medium, Arab Medium and Arab Heavy that are difficult to secure, the feedstock manager at the Ulsan-based refiner told Platts during a market engagement and Platts Market on Close (MOC) eWindow monitoring session on Oct. 29.
The growth in heavy sour Canadian crude shipments also supports South Korea's target of importing around 16 million barrels of Canadian crude in 2026, more than triple the 4.88 million barrels received in 2025.

South Korean refiners have continued to actively purchase WTI Midland and other US Gulf Coast grades despite high outright premiums and long-haul freight costs, valuing their consistent availability and lower exposure to the Strait of Hormuz shipping disruptions, feedstock managers at refiners based in Seosan and Ulsan said.
Platts assessed WTI Midland on a DES Yeosu basis at a premium of $26.80/b to front-month Dubai on Oct. 29, after reaching a record-high premium of $34.60/b on Sept. 18. On a CFR North Asia basis, however, WTI Midland averaged a $6.45/b discount to Abu Dhabi's Murban so far in 2026, reflecting elevated Persian Gulf-Asia freight and insurance costs.
Canadian crude arrivals rose to 1.73 million barrels in September, up 51.8% from August, taking January-September imports to around 12.6 million barrels, KNOC data showed.

Middle Eastern adjustments South Korea secured increased volumes of medium- and
light-sour grades with comparatively lower exposure to Strait of Hormuz disruptions. Imports of Murban, Upper Zakum, Das Blend and Umm Lulu contributed to September arrivals of 14.50 million barrels, up 44.5% year over year, while January-September shipments rose 14.7% to 116.06 million barrels, according to KNOC data, the feedstock manager at the Ulsan-based refiner told Platts during the MOC eWindow monitoring and market engagement session Oct. 29.
Oman Blend also continued to gain importance as refiners prioritized delivery certainty alongside feedstock economics. September imports reached 4.32 million barrels, up from 500,000 barrels a year earlier, lifting cumulative shipments during the first nine months to 20.73 million barrels, compared with 4.47 million barrels in the same period of 2025, KNOC data showed.

Nigeria's Seplat says on course for upper end of oil output guidance

Oman Blend's direct access to the Indian Ocean allows cargoes to reach Asian buyers without transiting the Strait of Hormuz, making the medium-sour grade an increasingly valuable alternative to supplies more exposed to Gulf shipping disruptions, the Seosan-based refinery feedstock manager said. South Korean refiners' feedstock procurement strategies have shifted toward securing guaranteed and timely deliveries, even when alternative grades carry higher premiums or freight costs, sweet and sour crude traders at two South Korean refiners' trading offices' in Singapore told Platts throughout Oct. 9-30.
The increased availability of Upper Zakum, Arab Light, and Oman Blend has helped South Korean refiners maintain access to staple medium-sour feedstocks, while reducing reliance on more disruption-sensitive supply routes. However, the country's complex refineries still require a broad crude slate, meaning these grades are complementing rather than fully replacing other Middle Eastern barrels, the feedstock managers at major refiners based in Ulsan, Seosan and Yeosu said.

-- Philip Vahn, Leon Wong and Charles Lee

Seplat Energy is on course to achieve its 2026 oil and gas production target of 155,000 barrels/day of oil equivalent after seeing output rise 7% year over year in the first nine months, the indigenous Nigerian producer said late Oct. 29.
In early 2026, Seplat set a full-year production target of 145,000-155,000 boe/d, having completed its acquisition of ExxonMobil's onshore and shallow water assets in December 2024.
Over the first three quarters of the year, production has averaged 144,999 boe/d, the company said in its statement, while Q3 production was 155,801 boe/d, up 13% year-on-year.
The company said its performance had been bolstered by reactivating wells abandoned by the US supermajor, with the Nigerian producer adding 33,700 b/d of oil and 176 MMcf/d of gas from 41 idle wells.
"I am delighted to report another quarter of robust production growth, averaging over 155,000 boe/d in Q2 2026, which puts us firmly on track to meet the upper half of our full year guidance," Seplat CEO Effiong Okon said.
"We have clear line of sight to continued growth that will be underpinned by operational excellence as offshore gas production comes back online."
The ExxonMobil deal gave Seplat a 40% stake in four Nigerian oil producing blocks -- OMLs 67, 68, 70 and 104 -- as well as the Qua terminal and the Bonny River terminal and Natural Gas Liquids plant.
Seplat said that strong oil output and higher global commodity prices during the period under review had allowed it to repay the $100 million balance of the debt it raised to acquire the ExxonMobil projects on Oct. 27.
A key asset acquired from the oil major, the prolific Yoho offshore field located in OML104, remains offline after efforts to restart the field failed due to pipeline damage, Seplat said. It added that the line is being repaired and is expected back online by the end of Q4 2026.
Restoring dormant and idle oil wells across the Niger Delta that were relinquished by international oil companies has formed a major plank of Nigeria's bid to raise crude output, while leaving majors to focus on deepwater projects.
It follows divestment deals by Shell, Eni, Equinor and others, in addition to ExxonMobil.
National production fell in previous years due to insecurity, theft and vandalism and underinvestment, particularly in exploration.
State upstream regulator the Nigerian Upstream Petroleum Regulatory Commission said Oct. 11 that it hoped to restore around 788,000 b/d of oil production previously shut-in by

At least 77 oil wells were brought back online between January and September, the commission said.
Nigeria's average oil production, including condensates, stood at 1.72 million b/d in September, according to government estimates.

Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
Trading activities on hold ahead of US midterm elections
Jan-Sept Americas crude imports hit record 191 million barrels
The Asia-Pacific sweet crude market on Oct. 30 saw muted trading activity, as market participants adopt a wait-and-see approach ahead of the US mid-term elections, regional traders said, with uncertainty over how the geopolitical landscape may unfold past Nov. 3 keeping markets on edge.
"Buyer[s are all] waiting for election results, now all in a conservative mode," a Singapore-based condensate trader said. "January-loading cycle will be super long with the overhang from the December-loading cycle," the trader added.
"The market increasingly seems to believe that whatever comes next is more likely to come after Nov. 3. That could be renewed diplomacy. It could be another round of strikes. What matters for crude is that traders now have to price both paths before Washington or Tehran has shown its hand." Quintex Global strategist, Stephen Innes, said Oct. 30.
Meanwhile, another Singapore-based crude trader added that the cash differentials of regional condensate cargoes could see further upsides from the late Oct. 29 attacks by the Iranianbacked Houthi militants on Saudi Arabian energy infrastructures, as South Korean end-users were heard to have procured various Middle Eastern condensate cargoes, according to various regional crude trade sources.
The Houthis claimed strikes on the 400,000 barrels/day Rabigh refinery and the 550,000 b/d Ras Tanura refinery, as well as Aramco facilities in Abqaiq, without specifying what was targeted there. Processing facilities at Abqaiq have been responsible for around 5% of the world's oil supply, according to information on Aramco's website. The facilities are located at the eastern end of the 7 million b/d East-West pipeline that carries crude to the Red Sea port of Yanbu.
Elsewhere, South Korea secured 191.4 million barrels of crude from suppliers in the Americas over January-September, marking record-high shipments from the region for the first three quarters, as Asia's top US crude buyer extended purchases alongside ample Canadian heavy crude inflows, according to industry sources, state-run Korea National Oil Corp., and the trade ministry.
The Americas accounted for 26.12% of South Korea's crude arrivals during the first three quarters, compared with 23.1% in

2025 and 21.6% in 2024, KNOC data issued Oct. 28 showed. The rising share reflects refiners' efforts to diversify feedstock
sources amid persistent geopolitical and logistical risks surrounding traditional Middle Eastern supply routes. Regular US export programs have provided dependable light sweet US barrels, while Canadian heavy sour grades have helped offset constrained availability of Arab Medium, Arab Heavy and other heavy Middle Eastern crudes, feedstock managers at three major refiners based in Ulsan, Seosan, Yeosu told Platts during market discussions over Oct. 28-30.
Over on the statistical data front, Singapore's crude oil imports fell 41.2% week over week to 662,422 mt over Oct. 22-28, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 29.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Alvinn Philips
Platts Middle East Sour Crude Daily Market Analysis
Dubai cash-futures spread averages $8.10/b in Oct, down $13.50/b MOM
Fresh Houthi attacks reported on Saudi facilities
Russian crudes see wide bid, offer spread in Chinese market
Middle East crude differentials ticked higher in the Oct. 30 session as the December-loading cycle drew to a close, though average monthly premiums were sharply weaker on the month.
Benchmark Platts cash Dubai saw its differential against same-month Dubai futures rise by $2.06/b to a $7.13/b premium at the Oct. 30 Asian close, snapping a 5-day decline. The spread had averaged a $8.10/b premium over October, down from $21.60/b over September.
On the Platts Market on Close assessment process over October, 24 convergence cargoes were declared, comprising 17 Oman crude cargoes and 7 Murban crude cargoes. Trafigura received 23 of those cargoes, while ExxonMobil took the remaining one cargo.
Spot activity remained mostly thin in the broader market, though fresh attacks on regional infrastructure sent outright Western crude oil markers to multi-month highs.
Yemen-based Houthi militants claimed a series of fresh missile strikes on Saudi Arabian energy facilities, including Abqaiq and two refineries, group spokesperson Ameen Hayyan said on X late Oct. 29. The Houthis claimed strikes on the 400,000 barrels/ day Rabigh refinery and the 550,000 b/d Ras Tanura refinery, as well as Aramco facilities in Abqaiq, without specifying what was targeted there.
In Russian crude trade, trade sources reported offers for December-arrival Urals or REBCO crude at premiums in the $23-$27/b range to ICE Brent crude futures on a DES Shandong basis.
Buying interest from Chinese refiners, meanwhile, was heard at premiums in the $14-$18/b range to ICE Brent, on a DES Shandong basis.

Platts Mediterranean & Black Sea Sweet Crude Daily Commentary
CPC trapped in the Med as NWE arbitrage closes
Saharan Blend valued below OSP levels
Potential upside in December limited: sources
Sweet grades in the Mediterranean closed the week under pressure Oct. 30 as falling differentials in the North Sea closed the arbitrage for CPC and steady flows from the US cap any potential upside for December cargoes.
"The arbitrage to Northwest Europe is closed at the moment, and any exports to the Far East are impossible," one CPC trader said.
With barrels still trapped in the region, the differentials remained under pressure, fetching double-digit discounts to Dated Brent benchmark for November-loading cargoes.
The freight remains expensive, particularly for the Suezmax vessels that carry most of CPC barrels from the Black Sea.
The arbitrage to the North opened earlier this week when higher prices for WTI Midland created space for CPC to be sold competitively, even if some refiners were not able to buy as much as they normally do.
"Some refineries are at the maximum of the mercaptans they can process, so they might buy only two cargoes of CPC Blend instead of three," another Med trader said.
Certain oil products, like jet fuel and naphtha, have strict limits on levels of mercaptans -- sulfur compounds that exist in higher proportion in CPC than other comparable sweet grades.
But the correction of the transatlantic freight combined with high US crude exports to Europe created a prompt overhang in the market. As Midland prices fell, the arbitrage for CPC closed, even though the Kazakh grade was offered down throughout the week.
North African sweets, Es Sider and Saharan Blend also remained under pressure, with the latter valued much below their official selling prices. Saharan was last heard below Dated Brent, while the OSP for November was set at $5.75/b premium, Platts reported earlier.
"There isn't much Saharan being traded at the moment, but I struggle to imagine it being priced at a premium [in the current environment]," a second Mediterranean trader said.
With freight costs still preventing barrels from leaving the region, the prompt market remains oversupplied.
"I'm not optimistic at all," a third Med source said. "As long as freight remains at this level, the region cannot absorb all the grades currently available."
-- Jan Kedzior

Oil NWE and Med Weekly Sour Crude Market Analysis
Johan Sverdrup corrects on ample supply
Med sour complex remains balanced
Asia pulls more Basrah Medium to replace Iranian supply
The European sour complex remained on a bearish trajectory over the week ended Oct. 30 amid ample supply in the North Sea, while the Mediterranean traded sideways week over week.
Platts assessed the Johan Sverdrup FOB Mongstad differential at a 79-cent/barrel discount to Dated Brent Oct. 29, down more than $2.50/b week over week to reach its lowest value since March 2 -- the onset of the US-Israel war with Iran. This was also the first time the differential had flipped into negative territory since Aug. 27.
Offering activity and collapsing values earlier in the week for Norway's Johan Sverdrup were originally attributed to weakness in the prompt, exacerbated by a high-freight environment.
However, the struggle to clear November barrels had persisted throughout the week, with falling offer levels reported by traders for third-decade loaders in tandem with a general weakening across the North Sea crude complex.
Trader pegs for end-November Johan Sverdrup values were reported as low as Dated Brent minus $3/b during Oct. 29 European afternoon trading, following earlier in the week indications closer to the $3/b mark.
Sentiment was dampened further by the release of the December loading program for the Norwegian grad, which will rise to 700,000 barrels/day, up from 536,667 b/d currently scheduled to load in November, according to a copy of the loading program seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 29.
The increase in volume is largely accounted for by the three VLCC and one Suezmax cargoes in the December program. These will be the first VLCC volumes scheduled since August and the first Suezmax since September.
In the Mediterranean, the market remained balanced, with prices edging higher week over week after Basrah Medium cargo arriving in December was heard at about a $3/b premium to December average Dated Brent, up from flat at the beginning of the week.
This put Basrah above Kirkuk, reversing a typical relationship where Kirkuk prices slightly above the seaborne Basrah.
"Some Med refiners are more comfortable with Basrah quality and just know it better so there is more potential upside," one Mediterranean-based trader said.
More Iraqi barrels have been flowing east as China has increased its purchases of Basrah Medium and Heavy to cover the shortfall in Iranian oil imports.
Iranian crude buyers were looking at Iraqi Basrah Medium and Basrah Heavy as alternatives to Iranian Light and Iranian Heavy grades, as the sanctioned barrels had almost dried up from the market, a Shandong-based trader said.
Still, October imports of Basrah Medium into the

Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
November cargoes see falling offer levels and value pegs
ICE Brent expiry activity limited as traders pull back on volatility
Supply pressure continued in the North Sea crude complex Oct. 30, with November cargoes repricing to find homes ahead of a looming December trading cycle, while ongoing market volatility kept activity associated with the expiration of the ICE Brent December 2026 futures muted.
With the market experiencing an overhang of North Sea cargoes, differentials would have to fall significantly for prompt cargoes to move, said a Europe-based North Sea crude trader.
"EU refiners cannot keep up with the supply," said a second trader.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process saw offers for November-loading Brent Blend, Forties and Oseberg cargoes -- all of which were left outstanding at the London close at premiums as low as $1.45/b over Dated Brent without finding a buyer.
Traders had also pegged values for November-loading cargoes significantly lower during Oct. 30 European afternoon trading, with values for Oseberg and Ekofisk heard as low as $2.50/b and Forties heard in negative territory.
WTI Midland CIF Rotterdam cargoes for November-arrival were also reported trading between $3-$4/b premiums in the session -- the last public offer for the grade was seen above the $4/b mark in the Oct. 29 MOC session.
While previous instances of an oversupplied complex had seen crude heading into storage to fill future demand, current contango levels across physical differentials and underlying Brent CFD contracts may need to steepen further in order to accommodate for a high cost of capital, said the first trader.
As a result of the falling levels for November cargoes, traders had reported bearish early activity for December cargoes, with buying interest coming in at lower levels than anticipated prior to the release of loading programs.
Meanwhile, activity across the intraday brokered market related to the expiration of the December 2026 ICE Brent futures contract and associated derivatives Oct. 30 was muted, with participants describing limited trading activity taking place.
"It's too volatile a market to do expiry, and difficult to have convictions before the [US] midterm elections and while the situation in the Straits remains unknown," said a third trader.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Natasha Tan

Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
WAF market between cycles with China heard buying from Brazi
Cash differentials remain in lower price set amid bearish fundamentals
The West African crude market remained in between trading cycles Oct. 30, with little movement reported by trading sources for the forthcoming December-loading cycle.
The main region of activity was heard to be from Chinese independent refineries buying WAF crudes at extremely softer values, according to one Europe-based WAF trader surveyed by Platts.
A second trader said that Chinese buyers were also returning to the market for Brazilian crudes, one of the WAF region's biggest competition for Asian buying, with crudes there heard to be landing at single-digit premiums to Dated Brent on a delivered basis.
Djeno crude from the Republic of Congo was heard to have mostly traded its November-loading cargoes, with last done values heard between $30/barrel and $25/b discounts to Dated Brent, several dollars below previously heard figures.
Sellers of the grade were said by a third trader to be offering what remained unsold on a delivered basis into China, pricing lower to compete with alternative crudes.
While market source said differentials were holding at lower levels, the region's FOB pricing was heard to have at least moved off the lows seen earlier in October owing to softer, though still elevated, freight costs into Europe and Asia.
Ghana's Jubilee crude was heard on the day to have seen a November clip last trade above a $10/b discount to Dated Brent, though a third WAF trader told Platts on Oct. 30 that the market for the grade was likely softer than when the trade was completed.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- George Delaney
Americas Crude
ExxonMobil eyes 2026 Permian oil output at 1.8 million boe/d
Three automated drilling rigs already on site
Studies underway for secondary recoveries
Chevron benefits from 25% less Permian capex
ExxonMobil Corp. is aiming to end the current year with an oil production of some 1.8 million barrels/day of oil equivalent in Texas' Permian Basin, on the back of its primary recoveries, even as the company initiates work on secondary recovery techniques, CEO Darren Woods said Oct. 30.
ExxonMobil is the only operator in the Permian that is using automated drilling rigs, Woods said during the

company's third-quarter 2026 earnings webcast, adding that it currently has three such rigs on site that are delivering some of the strongest drilling performance while significantly increasing safety.
"We expect to expand their use to about 25% of our Permian fleet next year and approximately half by 2028, helping us continue to grow US production safely and at a lower cost," he said.
ExxonMobil holds a leading acreage position in the Permian Basin and plans to increase production to over 2.5 million boe/d by 2030 and beyond, the company said in May 2026, noting at that time it was maintaining a cost of supply of $30/b or less to grow its shale oil production in that basin.
Technology will continue to play a pivotal role in improving recoveries from an unconventional reservoir - like the Permian Basin - currently estimated at 6% to 8%, it said then.
For its part, ExxonMobil is now "well on the way" to improving recoveries by 50% by the end of the decade, the company said, noting that its proprietary lightweight proppant offers significant potential to increase recoveries.
Incremental production at lower cost will come from primary recovery techniques, but work is also underway on secondary recovery, Woods said.
"The team is working on ideas for how we can improve secondary recovery. And I think again, in that space we are pretty enthusiastic. What I will tell you is [that] in order to maximize the opportunity set there, the primary recovery needs to be optimized," he said, pointing out that the benefits of such a process will likely be felt post-2030.
"You've got to think about secondary as you're developing your plans on the primary. And so my sense is that piece of the equation will start to manifest itself further down the line, probably much more materially in the outer years beyond 2030 than what we're seeing here," Woods said.
Separately, ExxonMobil's third-quarter 2026 global crude oil, NGL and natural gas production stood at 4.496 million boe/d, assuming a 350,000 boe/d impact from the Middle East situation, the company said in its earnings release.

Using industry chemicals and surfactants is boosting reservoir recoveries, and Chevron is putting that to use in the Permian and other shale plays in the US Lower 48 States, Wirth said.
"We've got a next generation of that chemistry that's moving out into field pilots, which we are optimistic may prove even more successful in terms of improving production outcomes and adding value. And we continue to look at other technologies related to drilling stimulation of the reservoir and different types of fracturing technologies, all of which will provide optionality into the future," Wirth said.
Extending output plateau Besides increasing recoveries, the new technologies and
applications has the potential to extend the economic life of the production plateau, Wirth said.
"They can take benches that are currently subeconomic and they can make them economic," he said. "And so we're certainly working on all of those."
In the near term, Chevron has seen a 25% reduction in its capital expenditure per barrel, besides lowering of operating costs, Wirth said.
"We're seeing reductions in downtime events. We're seeing a tremendous amount of innovation and application of new tools and technology to get more value out of this 1 million b/d business," Wirth said.

Chevron holds output at 1 million boe/d Meanwhile, fellow producer Chevron Corp. is holding a plateau
production of 1 million boe/d from its acreages in the Permian Basin -- for the sixth consecutive quarter -- as it benefits from lowering costs and increasing efficiencies, CFO Eimear Bonner said on the company's third-quarter 2026 earnings webcast.
"We had signaled the intent to bring the Permian to a plateau in kind of the middle of the last decade, to be honest when we were [at] pretty modest production levels, CEO Mike Wirth said on the same webcast.
"And as we reached and surpassed 1 million b/d, we turned to managing that plateau, which will last not only the end of this decade, but the end of the next. And I expect well beyond that as we apply more technology to this enormous resource base," Wirth said.

Imperial Oil cuts Kearl output target after pipeline outage
Imperial cuts Kearl 2026 target to 280,000 b/d Transformer failure halts output mid-September Q3 upstream production rises 4,000 boe/d
Imperial Oil Ltd. has lowered its 2026 gross production target for Kearl to 280,000 b/d following third-party disruptions, including a pipeline outage that halted output for several days in the third quarter, CEO John Whelan said Oct. 30.
A transformer failure affecting the Woodland Pipeline halted Kearl production for several days in mid-September before

October 30, 2026

takeaway capacity was restored. The revised annual target is about 5,000 b/d below the
low end of Kearl's previous guidance, Whelan, also Imperial's chairman and president, said during the company's third-quarter earnings call. Cold Lake and Syncrude remain on track for the low end of their respective annual guidance ranges.
Imperial's third-quarter upstream production averaged 418,000 gross barrels of oil equivelant per day, up 4,000 boe/d from the second quarter. Kearl output rose 25,000 b/d sequentially to 282,000 b/d gross as the absence of its secondquarter turnaround more than offset the pipeline disruption, Whelan said. Kearl also reached a record 1 billion barrels of cumulative production during the quarter.
Cold Lake production fell 10,000 b/d sequentially to 139,000 b/d, reflecting the Mahihkan turnaround, which began in September and finished ahead of schedule in October.
Leming's steam-assisted gravity drainage project was producing 10,000 b/d, while Grand Rapids' solvent-assisted SAGD project was exceeding expectations at more than 21,000 b/d, Whelan said.
Imperial's share of Syncrude production averaged 70,000 b/d, down 3,000 b/d from the second quarter because of a nowcompleted coker turnaround.
Imports of bitumen and gas oil through the interconnect pipeline supported upgrader utilization and added about 10,000 b/d of production on Imperial's share, Whelan said.
Upstream investment focused on sustaining operations at Kearl, Syncrude and Cold Lake. Work during the quarter included heavy equipment deliveries and project milestones at Kearl, as well as mine progression activities at Syncrude, said Dan Lyons, senior vice president, finance and administration.
Kearl has started its flotation-column secondary recovery project, which recovers additional bitumen from ore already processed through the plant.
Whelan said the project and longer intervals between major turnarounds support the target of 300,000 b/d in 2028. No major Kearl turnaround is planned in 2027 or 2028, with the next turnaround scheduled for 2029.
Cheryl Gomez-Smith, senior vice president of upstream, said Kearl will begin producing from its new East pit in 2027 alongside the North pit. Delineation work indicates stronger ore quality at East pit, she said, while engineered roads and greater diversity of ore headings should support productivity and reliability.
Outlook With planned turnarounds completed, Imperial is aiming to
maximize fourth-quarter output. "No planned shutdowns, and that's what we're looking to do,"
Whelan said. When asked about Kearl's ability to deliver a nearrecord finish, he said: "We have to deliver that, but we feel the capability is there to do it."
Gomez-Smith said Kearl had advanced mine sequencing to increase plant feed, staged more ripping dozers for winter and expanded its engineered road network.

Whelan added that the year's disruptions had not altered confidence in future production targets, including 300,000 b/d at Kearl and 165,000 b/d at Cold Lake. "We don't see systemic issues," he said.
Longer term, Whelan said Imperial's undeveloped resource base offers the potential to double gross operated production.
Greater fiscal certainty and regulatory reform could unlock significant industry investment, he said, citing Canadian policy announcements aimed at supporting growth and improving project approval timelines.
Lyons also pointed to a proposed productivity tax deduction that, if enacted as expected, would accelerate tax benefits for eligible capital assets acquired and available for use after Sept. 14. He said the measure would support future cash generation.
Imperial is also considering bringing forward a final investment decision on Aspen from the end of the decade, potentially using solvent-assisted SAGD for an initial phase, Whelan said.
Stronger-than-expected Grand Rapids performance has reinforced confidence in that technology, although enhanced bitumen recovery remains the preferred long-term approach.
"So instead of that FID at the end of the decade, we are looking at, do we want to bring it forward?" he said.
The enhanced bitumen recovery pilot at Aspen remains on track to start in early 2027 and would take two to three years to prove the technology, Whelan said. Any acceleration would depend on technology readiness, project economics and the fiscal and regulatory environment.
He envisaged sequencing Aspen and subsequent developments rather than building Aspen, Clark Creek and Corner simultaneously, but no accelerated investment decision was announced.

PetroChina, CNOOC one cargo each
Brazilian government subsalt management company PPSA sold 3 million barrels of crude from the Buzios productionsharing field to state-led oil company Petrobras, PetroChina and China National Offshore Oil Corp. at the regulator's 10th spot market oil auction held Oct. 28, according to preliminary results for the sale.
PetroChina claimed the first lot, which included a single cargo of 1 million barrels from the FPSO P-79 floating production, storage and offloading vessel, PPSA said in a statement Oct. 29 after markets closed. The cargo is scheduled to be offloaded in the first half of December.
CNOOC submitted the top offer for the second lot, which featured a single cargo of 1 million barrels from the FPSO Almirante Tamandare, PPSA said. The cargo is scheduled to be offloaded in the first half of December.

Buzios was the top production-sharing oil producer at 865,510 b/d in August, up 5.3% from 822,100 b/d in July, PPSA said. Brazil's National Petroleum Agency, meanwhile, said Oct. 1 that Buzios produced 1.172 million b/d in August, remaining above 1 million b/d for a third consecutive month, according to the regulator.

BRAZIL DATA: September crude oil exports soar to record monthly high on subsalt output growth
Tops March 2023 record
Offsets 12% export tax
Product imports decline
Brazilian crude oil exports soared to a fresh record monthly high in September as growing output from offshore subsalt fields and the global search for secure energy supplies offset a 12% tax implemented in March, according to the National Petroleum Agency, or ANP.
Oil companies operating in Brazil shipped a record 83.552 million barrels in September, an increase of 42.2% from 58.752 million barrels in September 2025, the ANP said in a report

published Oct. 29. That topped the previous monthly export record of 78.649 million barrels set in March 2023. September's oil exports also climbed 39.8% from 59.744 million barrels in August.
The surge in overseas oil shipments came amid a series of production records at offshore oil fields in the subsalt frontier, where state-led oil company Petrobras and its partners have installed several floating production units at the Buzios and Mero production sharing fields over the past two years. Equipment upgrades and modernization programs have also added about 180,000 b/d of nominal production capacity at existing platforms in 2026.
The record oil output offset the impact of a 12% tax on crude exports that was implemented by the government in March. The tax was aimed at reducing outflows and mitigating the impact of volatile international oil and refined product prices on Latin America's largest economy. The tax was extended by 60 days on Sept. 3.
Brazil previously implemented a 9.2% tax on oil exports after Russia invaded Ukraine in February 2022. Oil companies filed lawsuits disputing the export tax, which a judge ruled unconstitutional in 2025 and ordered the government to reimburse oil exporters. Oil companies, however, have so far failed to overturn the current export tax.
Despite the export tax, Brazil remains on pace to reach record oil exports for a fourth consecutive year in 2026, the ANP report showed. Year to date through September, Brazil's oil exports were up 10.1% year over year to 563.4 million barrels. Brazil set a record for oil exports in 2025 at 701.8 million barrels.
Oil imports, meanwhile, were mixed in September, the ANP said. Brazil has historically imported heavier grades and condensates for lubricants production and refinery blending.
Brazil imported 6.614 million barrels in September, down 10.6% from 7.397 million barrels in September 2025, the ANP said. September's crude imports, however, were up 11.9% from 5.906 million barrels in August.
Refined product imports decline The ANP report showed that higher domestic refinery output,
government fuel subsidies and weaker agricultural demand during the peak oilseed and sugarcane harvest seasons reduced the need for imported refined products.
Brazil has historically imported about 25% of diesel demand and 15% of gasoline demand, according to the ANP. Imported barrels have been partially offset by higher biofuel blending mandates in recent years. Brazil increased the volume of anhydrous ethanol blended with gasoline sold at the pump to 32% from 30% on Aug. 1. The country also blends 15% biodiesel into diesel sold at the pump.
Brazil imported 18.275 million barrels of refined products in September, down 17.7% from 22.21 million barrels in September 2025, the ANP said. September's refined product imports also fell 5.1% from 19.249 million barrels in August.
Diesel imports were mixed in September at 8.08 million barrels,

down 26.5% from 10.995 million barrels in September 2025, the ANP said. September's diesel imports, however, rose 5.9% from 7.627 million barrels in August.
Gasoline imports were also mixed in September, the ANP said. Brazil imported 2.301 million barrels of gasoline in September, more than double the 996,480 barrels imported in September 2025. September's gasoline imports, however, were down 35.7% from 3.581 million barrels in August.
Refined product exports, meanwhile, were broadly lower in September, the ANP said. Brazil exported 10.423 million barrels of refined products, down 9.1% from 11.469 million barrels in September 2025. September's product imports also sank 24.9% from 13.875 million barrels in August.
Bunker fuel and fuel oil shipments led refined product exports, but were broadly lower, according to the ANP. Brazil exported 6.181 million barrels in September, down 23.4% from 8.07 million barrels in September 2025. September's exports also tumbled 31.1% from 8.974 million barrels in August.

Brazil's Petrobras makes third oil discovery at Morpho well in Foz do Amazonas
Unlocks equatorial margin frontier
Follows finds in August, October
Nearly 600 blocks on offer in 2027
Brazilian state-led oil company Petrobras has confirmed a third oil discovery in the Morpho exploration well in the offshore Foz do Amazonas Basin, completing drilling work on the wildcat that unlocked hydrocarbons deposits across three separate intervals.
"Our technical persistence allowed us to make three discoveries in the same well, which leaves us even more enthused about the exploration potential of the equatorial margin," Petrobras CEO Magda Chambriard said in a statement Oct. 29 after markets closed. Petrobras previously announced oil discoveries at Morpho on Aug. 14 and Oct. 2.
The latest discovery also underscored Chambriard's comments Oct. 2 during a press conference to discuss the second find at Morpho, when the executive indicated that evaluations of drilling samples were ongoing despite finally reaching the well's target depth. Chambriard said at the time that there "was still room" to find something more.
The real work has now been completed at Morpho, with the conclusion of "drilling and geological data acquisition," Petrobras said. The company still needs to complete operations related to proper abandonment of the exploration well.
Once the abandonment process is complete, the ODN-II drillship will be demobilized and relocated to the Potiguar Basin offshore Rio Grande do Norte state, Petrobras said. The drillship, which is also known as NS-42, is expected to start drilling the Mae de Ouro prospect in November.

Petrobras, meanwhile, expects to return to drilling in the region in the first half of 2027, according to the company. The Constellation-operated Amaralina Star drillship is expected to head toward the FZA-M-59 block in the first quarter. The Amaralina Star, also known as NS-43, is scheduled to drill three contingent wells in the block aimed at determining the size of Morpho.
The Brazilian Institute for the Environment and Renewable Natural Resources, or IBAMA, expanded Petrobras' drilling permit in the block to include the three contingent wells on Sept. 3. Petrobras started drilling Morpho in October 2025, days after IBAMA granted the initial drilling permit.
The Morpho extension well will be drilled slightly to the northeast of the initial Morpho discovery well, according to IBAMA's coordinates. The Crotalus well, meanwhile, will target a prospect in the northwest section of the FZA-M-59 block. The Manga well will target a prospect to the southwest of the initial Morpho discovery well.
Equatorial margin expansion Brazil's National Petroleum Agency, or ANP, expects interest
in the country's equatorial margin to increase after Petrobras' discoveries and recent expansion of the country's continental shelf. Morpho was the first major exploration drilled in the mostpromising area of the country's equatorial margin in more than 15 years.
The ANP plans to add 587 exploration and production blocks in the equatorial margin to the regulator's Open Acreage concession program portfolio in the first half of 2027, according to officials. That would more than double the current portfolio of 446 blocks.
Geologists consider the Pelotas Basin on Brazil's maritime border with Uruguay and the equatorial margin that extends from Amapa state in the west to Rio Grande do Norte state in the east as the country's two remaining exploration frontiers. The two areas are believed to hold similar deposits as those found offshore Guyana, Ghana, Namibia, South Africa and Suriname in recent years.
Brazil needs to rebuild its oil and natural gas reserves, with output from subsalt production sharing fields expected to peak in the early to mid-2030s. The country currently has enough reserves to sustain production for 12.5 years, according to the ANP. That has raised concerns that Latin America's largest economy could return to the ranks of net oil importers for the first time since achieving energy independence in 2014.
Petrobras also plans to expand its exploration activity beyond the FZA-M-59 block, submitting permits to drill an additional seven wells in other blocks in the waters offshore Amapa state.
Petrobras holds stakes in a total of 16 blocks in the Foz do Amazonas Basin, including 100% operating stakes in six blocks acquired during the ANP's 11th bid round held in 2013. Petrobras and ExxonMobil, meanwhile, teamed to acquire 10 blocks during the ANP's fifth Open Acreage concession cycle held in June 2025.

Democratic Congress could cool some oil investment in Venezuela: analysts
NABEP agreement faces Democratic pressure
Oversight may chase some small players away
Democratic control of one or both houses of Congress after the midterm elections is expected to boost oversight of US involvement in Venezuela's oil sector, which could cool some oil investment there, energy analysts say.
"Congressional scrutiny could have a significant impact on investment in Venezuela," David Goldwyn, chair of the Atlantic Council Global Energy Center's Energy Advisory Group, told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Traditional investors like Chevron will be fine, and projects that align with Venezuela's new hydrocarbons law and regulations should be secure, Goldwyn said. Both Democrats and Republicans want Venezuela to be self-sustaining, and foreign investment is essential for that, he said.
But a Democratic Congress would likely scrutinize idiosyncratic arrangements like the US deal with North America Blue Energy Partners (NABEP), focusing on why and how the company was chosen, Goldwyn said.
A Democratic Congress would also have an interest in much greater transparency about where Venezuela oil revenues are going, how they are spent, and whether the US licensing policy is equitable, Goldwyn said.
Rachel Ziemba, a senior adviser with Horizon Engage, also said the biggest risk from oversight is for the NABEP deal. Congress is likely to push for more clarity on what the deal allows and increase oversight over the US government stake, she said.
In August 2026, the US Departments of State and Defense signed an agreement with NABEP, giving the US a 35% interest in the company and preferential access to purchase 20% production at cost.
NABEP, which retains operational control, has the rights to commercialize more than 65 billion barrels of proven oil reserves in Venezuela and plans to invest nearly $100 billion to increase production capacity and improve oil and gas infrastructure, the company says. The company has increased its crude oil output to more than 200,000 barrels per day, compared with 18,000 b/d two years ago with an investment of some $1 billion, NABEP said Oct. 13.

NABEP deal faces scrutiny Lawmakers have already begun seeking more information
about the deal. On Sept. 15, Representative Joaquin Castro (Democrat-Texas),
the ranking member of the House Foreign Affairs Subcommittee on the Western Hemisphere, motioned to subpoena federal agencies and private companies for records on the NABEP deal. The motion failed 6-6 along party lines.
On Sept. 30, Democratic Senators Jeanne Shaheen, Jack Reed, Martin Heinrich, and Elizabeth Warren, wrote to the Trump administration demanding information about the deal. Among other details, they sought the complete agreement between the US and NABEP, the legal authority for US involvement, and whether any member of the Trump administration stands to benefit financially from the arrangement.
The senators raised concern that NABEP has only two years of experience in the oil industry, with no evidence that it has the operational and financial capacity to provide the investment needed to bring the deal to fruition. They also argued that the statute creating the Department of Defense's Office of Strategic Capital does not explicitly provide the authority to take an equity stake in a private oil company, according to the letter.
When asked about congressional oversight and the senators' letter, a NABEP spokesperson said that the company is not a political entity and is instead focused on boosting oil production.
Output growth is driven by a team of Venezuelan engineers who bring 30+ years of experience in the country's oil fields, and by the hundreds of millions of dollars the company reinvests in output every month, the spokesperson said."
"NABEP increased production tenfold in our first two years, backed by $1 billion in company-funded investment," the spokesperson said. "In just the few weeks since the deal between the US government and NABEP was signed on August 31st, we've already grown production by another 10%, an additional 20,000 barrels per day."
Secretary of State Marco Rubio said that the fact that NABEP is partnered with the US gives the company the certainty needed to make massive investments.
"It's created the market confidence that they can now go out and raise the money to bring in the capital investment to increase the productive capacity of Venezuela," Rubio told reporters during

an Oct. 26 teleconference. Venezuela state oil company PDVSA and its foreign partners
produced 1.218 million b/d of crude in September, according to preliminary data from the Ministry of Hydrocarbons reviewed by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
PDVSA estimates that Venezuela will reach 1.3 million b/d of production by the end of 2026, and 1.5 million b/d by the end of 2027, according to Hector Obregón, the company's president.
Other oversight risks remain Congress is unlikely to do much to change the administration's
direction of travel on Venezuela, but increased scrutiny could still lead to headline risk for oil firms, Ziemba said
Companies may be worried about disclosures that could affect shareholders and other stakeholders, or about making other investment terms public, she said. Congressional inquiries could also eat up legal fees and time, and information disclosures could increase the risk of civil cases, she said.
Kenneth Medlock, senior director of the Center for Energy Studies at Rice University, said congressional scrutiny could affect investment by some of the smaller players in Venezuela.
Smaller producers, who are primarily wealthy individuals seeking to grow their production, may come under scrutiny, Medlock said. Congressional hearings and investigations could chase some of those players away, he said.
But larger companies are making decisions based on expected 10-, 20-, and 30-year commercial outcomes and will make their decisions based on risk and profitability, Medlock said. There is hope that true democracy will emerge in Venezuela, along with the rule of law and contract sanctity, Medlock said. If that happens, the larger companies will likely look to expand their footprint, he said.
"In terms of elections having an outcome in Venezuela, if one happens in Venezuela that results in the re-establishment of true democracy, it will have a much larger impact than anything that happens in November in the US," Medlock said.
Speaking after a fourth round of talks on the political transition process in Venezuela, a senior State Department official said that reforms of Venezuelan institutions will be completed in the coming months to enable a credible presidential election.
A new slate of magistrates for Venezuela's Supreme Justice Tribunal will be selected by the end of the year, and reforms and membership selection for Venezuela's National Electoral Council will be completed shortly thereafter, the official said during a separate Oct. 26 briefing.
Once those reforms are in place, Interim President Delcy Rodríguez will declare a vacancy in the presidency, triggering a six-month period for new elections, the official said. "You have to create the architecture for a credible election before you can actually call for one or have one," the official said.

CNOOC maintains 2026 production target despite higher costs
Lingshui 17-2 platform to restart in Q4
Guyana output to reach 1.3 million b/d by 2030
All-in cost rises 11.8% to $30.57/boe
CNOOC maintained its 2026 oil and gas production target at 780 million-800 million barrels of oil equivalent, or about 2.14 million-2.19 million boe/d, company executives said during its third-quarter results call Oct. 30.
The company's production rose 2.7% year over year to 2.17 million boe/d in the first nine months of the year, supported by stable output from mature fields and incremental production from new projects, the executives said.
Work to resume production at the Lingshui 17-2 platform was progressing in an orderly manner, with production expected to restart in the fourth quarter, according to one of the executives. The company expects the outage to have a limited impact on its full-year production.
In Guyana, four projects were already onstream and another four projects were awaiting start-up. The eight projects are expected to produce an average of about 900,000 b/d, with block output projected to reach 1.3 million b/d by 2030, the executive said, adding that the block is being developed under a production-sharing contract.
CNOOC remains confident it will secure deepwater Blocks 24 and 25 in Trinidad and Tobago and proceed with investment, as talks with the government are progressing smoothly, according to the executive.
Earlier in 2026, the Trinidad and Tobago government approved CNOOC's bids for the two blocks, although the production-sharing contracts have yet to be finalized. The deepwater gas blocks are located in the Tobago Basin, according to an S&P Global Energy upstream assessment dated July 2026.
CNOOC's all-in cost per barrel of oil equivalent rose 11.8% year over year to $30.57/boe in the first nine months of 2026, mainly as a stronger yuan increased the dollar-equivalent value of its yuan-denominated domestic costs, a second executive said.
Higher oil prices also led to a significant increase in taxes, while changes in the production mix across different fields contributed to the rise. Excluding the impact of taxes, exchange rates and other factors, the company said costs remained effectively controlled.

Peru signs contract with Colombian Green Energy for northern onshore oil Block 206
Colombian Green Energy to drill 1 exploration well
Contract includes 40 development wells, 81 workovers
SPT Energy, Cheiron also secured blocks this week
Peru's government signed a contract with Colombian Green Energy for northern onshore Block 206, its third E&P deal this week.
Under the 30-year contract, the Bogota-based company will drill one exploration well, 40 development wells and carry out 81 workovers at the 17,178-hectare concession in the Talara Basin, state oil contracting agency Perupetro said in a statement late Oct. 29.
Oil blocks on the north coast have traditionally supplied crude to the nearby state-owned 95,000 b/d Talara refinery. The contract follows agreements by Singapore-based SPT Energy and Egypt's Cheiron for blocks in northern Peru earlier this week, as the government seeks to attract investment to reverse declining crude output. A unit of Colombia's Omega Energy last week also signed a technical evaluation agreement for an area in the northern jungle Maranon Basin.
Peru's hydrocarbons industry, which saw investment fall to $564 million last year from a peak of $1.88 billion in 2012, has pending bid rounds for offshore Block Z-69 and northern jungle Blocks 67 and 39. State oil company Petroperu, a separate entity, is also in talks with potential partners for jungle Block 64.
Peru, whose crude production hit a 10-year high in 2019, has yet to recover to pre-pandemic levels as companies including Perenco, Frontera Energy and Pluspetrol have pulled out of northern jungle blocks.
Peru averaged 39,458 b/d through September, according to Perupetro.

ARA gasoline stocks rebound as naphtha inventories fall: Insights Global
Naphtha stocks drop 25.62% on demand surge
Petrochemical buying tightens prompt supply

Gasoline inventories in the ARA hub rose 22.77% week over week to 868,000 mt, according to the data published on Oct. 29.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed FOB ARA Eurobob gasoline barges down $28.75/mt at $1,042/mt on Oct. 29, while

the equivalent cash differential narrowed $8/mt to $6/mt, its lowest since March 30.
Market participants pointed to healthy refinery runs and sufficient availability of finished gasoline in Europe, with one source saying cargoes remained available and there was no real scarcity of gasoline.
Despite the volume available, trading activity was described as muted. A market source said the sharp decline in cash differentials and weaker backwardation had reduced participants' incentive to offer barrels aggressively at current levels.
Export opportunities also remained limited. Another market source saw limited near-term pull from the US amid stockbuilding there, while high freight costs continued to challenge the economics of moving European barrels between regional and export markets.
High freight and blending-component costs were also complicating gasoline economics. Market sources pointed to expensive MTBE and butane, while one source said buying finished-grade gasoline currently made considerably more economic sense than blending.
Platts assessed the front-month gasoline-naphtha spread at $91.75/mt on Oct. 29, down $7.75/mt day over day.
Naphtha stocks draw amid strong buying The naphtha market presented a contrasting picture, with
ARA stocks declining 25.62% week over week to 270,000 mt. The draw extended the year-over-year deficit, with inventories 43.04% below the 474,000 mt recorded in late October 2025.
European naphtha market sources pointed to tight prompt availability and strong petrochemical buying, with healthy eastbound arbitrage also supporting the regional market.
"Naphtha in Europe is fairly supported because petrochemical demand is very strong," a trading source said. "And Eastern arbs are healthy."
The trading source also pointed to constrained propane availability supporting additional naphtha demand from the petrochemical sector.
"Some refineries are running propane because TTF is very expensive to run for making power," the trading source said. "That leaves some extra small pockets of demand for naphtha into the petrochemical sector."
Softer gasoline blending demand has meanwhile reduced competition for naphtha barrels from the gasoline pool, according to market sources, while petrochemical demand has remained supportive.
"So at the moment the petrochemical sector is keeping the naphtha from falling, especially in Europe, but all the other products, like gasoline or gasoil, are all moving down/demand is weakening," the trading source said.
Platts assessed physical CIF Northwest European naphtha cargoes at $970.25/mt on Oct. 29, down $14.50/mt day over day. The front-month CIF NWE naphtha swap fell $13/mt to $944.50/ mt, while the equivalent crack strengthened 66 cents/b to minus $3.90/b.

ARA-origin gasoline leads Brazil's import in October
Netherlands supplies 45% of gasoline volume Petrobras imports 183,220 cubic meters Suape port receives most discharge activity

Brazil is expected to receive at least 361,113 cubic meters of gasoline imports through Oct. 28, according to preliminary data from port lineups, sources, and S&P Global Commodities at Sea on Oct. 30.
Despite total gasoline imports of 316,113 cubic meters in October, 183,220 cubic meters were attributed to state-owned Petrobras. This means that private-sector imports totaled approximately 177,893 cubic meters, excluding Petrobras volumes.
At least nine ships are scheduled and confirmed to discharge imported gasoline at Brazilian ports by October. Most will already have arrived, berthed, or discharged.

The Brazilian gasoline import arbitrage window has been closed for 247 days, according to an Oct. 30 report from the Brazilian Association of Fuels Importers, Abicom. Across Brazil's six primary transport fuel trading hubs, domestic prices average about 640 reais/cubic meter below import-parity prices.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Suape all-origin gasoline at $2.7931/gallon, with ARA as the most competitive origin, at a 29.5-cent discount to NYMEX December RBOB.
-- Beatriz Correr

Uruguay to keep diesel, gasoline reference prices unchanged in Nov
Prices stay unchanged from October Aims to cushion impact on drivers Otherwise, diesel would rise over 30% on month

The Netherlands is set to be the top origin, accounting for 186,221 cubic meters, or 45% of October imports, underscoring the prevalence of products from the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp hub. Russia is the second-largest importer with 127,893 cubic meters (31%), and Spain with 98,457 (24%).
Despite Russia's ban on fuel exports to secure domestic needs, three cargoes of Russia-origin gasoline appear as arriving in Brazil. Those are exported from Russia as naphtha and are blended in transit to meet Brazilian A-grade gasoline specifications, which have low octane numbers due to the mandatory final 32% anhydrous ethanol blend in the country.
For these international gasoline imports, the top two discharge ports are Suape (187,325 cubic meters) and Itaqui (123,789 cubic meters).
According to preliminary data, gasoline imports will reach 201,926 cubic meters by Nov. 12, with cargoes from the ARA region, Russia and the US scheduled to arrive at Brazil's main ports. Import volumes are expected to rise further ahead of the country's peak driving season in December and January.

Uruguay said Oct. 30 it will keep diesel and gasoline reference prices unchanged in November from October to ease the impact of high global crude prices on drivers.
Pump prices will remain at 90.45 pesos/liter ($2.24/l) for 95 RON gasoline and 62.79 pesos/l for diesel 50, the Office of the Presidency said in a statement.
The decision "will cushion the impact on consumers," the office said.
If not, 95 RON gasoline would rise 3.3% month over month in November and diesel 50 31.3%, the office added.
The government imposed the freeze on reference prices in April after the US-Iran war drove up international crude prices. The strategy has mitigated the impact of international market fluctuations on domestic prices since April, helping to "provide greater stability for consumers," the office said.
Brent prices touched $118/b on Oct. 30 on concerns about global supply disruptions as the Middle East conflict continues. That is well above the $65/b average before the war started at the end of February.
Uruguay's state-owned oil firm Ancap imports crude to process at its 50,000 b/d La Teja refinery, the country's only refinery.
-- Charles Newbery

European gasoline-naphtha pressured by weak blending demand, tighter naphtha supply
Front-month gasoline-naphtha spread falls 71% to a seven-month low
Forward curve flips into contango as gasoline blending economics weakens
European gasoline-naphtha spreads have continued to fall sharply as weakening gasoline fundamentals and tighter naphtha availability eroded blending economics, with forward structures signaling continued pressure into early 2027.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed the frontmonth gasoline-naphtha spread at $91.50/metric ton on Oct. 28, down $7.75/mt day over day and 71.07% month over month. The December spread fell $6/mt day over day to $102.75/mt.
The gasoline-naphtha spread fell to a seven-month low amid subdued gasoline blending demand, with activity expected to remain quiet until mid-November.
"Gas-nap [was] hammered," a player active in the gasoline blendstocks market said. "Blending activity seems quiet until mid-November."
The narrower spread has weakened the economics of blending naphtha into gasoline, reducing competition for barrels from the gasoline pool and improving their availability to petrochemical buyers.
Gasoline stocks build as naphtha inventories fall The shift comes alongside diverging inventory trends for
the two products. Insights Global data showed AmsterdamRotterdam-Antwerp gasoline stocks rose 22.8% week over week to 868,000 mt in the week ended Oct. 29, recovering from a fiveyear low, from September 2021, while naphtha stocks fell 25.6% to 270,000 mt over the same period.
Gasoline market participants have pointed to healthy refinery runs and ample availability of finished-grade gasoline, while immediate export opportunities have remained limited. One market source said cargoes remained available with no real scarcity of gasoline, despite the recent weakness in cracks and market structure.
High freight and blending-component costs have further challenged blending economics. Another market source said buying finished-grade gasoline currently made considerably more economic sense than blending, while elevated MTBE costs made non-oxygenated gasoline more attractive.
"Reformate prices are low now, so reforming margins are lower too. [We] need cheaper feedstock," the blendstocks market participant said.
Export demand offers limited support Meanwhile, gasoline export demand has remained
constrained by uneconomic transatlantic arbitrage. US Atlantic Coast motor gasoline inventories rose 2.3 million barrels to 53.4 million barrels, further limiting expectations for near-term

European gasoline demand from the US, according to US Energy Information Administration data published Oct. 28.
For much of 2026, strong gasoline margins drew substantial naphtha volumes into the blending pool, while weak cracker economics and cheaper LPG kept petrochemical demand subdued. That balance has begun to shift as seasonal weakness in gasoline erodes blending economics and reduces gasolinesector demand for naphtha.
"Hearing more blending barrels ending up in the petchem pull," a trading source said.
Forward curve signals continued pressure The changing economics have also become increasingly
visible along the forward curve, with gasoline-naphtha structures recently flipping into contango.
The November/December spread narrowed $1.75/mt to minus $11.25/mt on Oct. 28, down $14.50/mt week over week. The December/January structure stood at minus $10/mt, while January/February was at minus $26/mt.
The forward structure reflects expectations for continued naphtha strength into the end of the year and early 2027, while seasonally lower gasoline demand has weighed on gasoline values further along the curve.
Despite the weakness in prompt gasoline, forward cracks showed expectations of some recovery into the first quarter. Platts assessed the FOB ARA Eurobob first quarter of 2027 crack at $17.48/barrel on Oct. 26, $3.13/b above the equivalent November crack.
Gasoline market participants also pointed to first-quarter refinery maintenance as a potential source of upside risk to gasoline margins following the recent sell-off.
-- Friedrich Gutsche, Barbara Fernandez-pita
Japan's cabinet approves $7.28B for gasoline subsidy fund
Subsidies hit record ¥52.4/liter in October
Target price rises ¥5 monthly from December
The Japanese government, led by Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi, on Oct. 30 approved allocating ¥1.15 trillion ($7.28 billion) to a gasoline subsidy fund from a reserve fund for responses to the Middle East situations at a Cabinet meeting, Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry Ryosei Akazawa said at a press briefing in Tokyo Oct. 30.
"The government will seek to make the subsidy program more sustainable, while keeping a close eye on people's lives, economic activities and crude oil prices," Akazawa reiterated what Takaichi said Oct. 29.
Takaichi announced Oct. 29 that the government will begin raising its gasoline target price of about ¥170/liter by ¥5/l every month from December through early spring.
"The government secures a budget for the gasoline subsidy till December," Nobuhiko Kikkawa, director of fuel distribution
